# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 100

Making. The Gender Action Plan specifically describes opportunities for gender mainstreaming in the project design so that the proposed project interventions address gendered climate resilience across the household, community and institutional levels.

215. Haiti ranks third globally on the Long-Term Climate Risk Index 142, demonstrating its extreme vulnerability to climate change — to which women are especially vulnerable. Widespread deforestation and soil degradation have so severely impacted the island’s environment that it can no longer provide critical ecosystem functions, such as mitigating the effects of heavy rain and flooding. This has progressively increased human vulnerability to climate change. Extreme climate events, such as tropical storms and hurricanes, are expected to increase in frequency and intensity, while rainfall is expected to become more erratic across seasons 143. In an already denuded and degraded landscape, this increased rainfall engenders severe erosion and landslides, flash flooding, and sedimentation of river channels 144.

216. Climate change impacts are not gender-neutral and many of the consequences of climate-induced impacts are more severe for women, and other socio-economically marginalised groups, given their specific livelihood circumstances. In relative terms, women lack access to productive resources, and this has impacts on their health, food security and safety in the context of flooding and other extreme climate events. The additional socio-cultural barriers limiting participation and movement outside the household sphere is worsened by phenomena such as flooding, drought and erratic rainfall. These constraints cause women to have to work much harder to secure food and water, and generate additional income through livelihoods which, in turn, diminishes their ability to advance out of poverty, particularly when they have, for example, lost their land due to the impacts of flooding during cyclone events.

217. Gender equality and women’s empowerment are not addressed in most of Haiti’s strategy or action plan documents for climate change and disaster risk management 145, 146. Flooding has cumulative livelihood effects that unfairly impact women and exacerbate gender inequality. In Haiti, women are disproportionately represented among the poor and there is a significant portion of women-headed households, so impacts such as crop loss, animal drowning, destroyed homes, health risks or displacement and loss of life resulting from floods ultimately affect women to a higher extent. Yet, during these extreme flooding events, the importance of grassroots organisation and mobilisation of women’s groups during disaster relief efforts has been unparalleled and critical, demonstrating the resilience of Haitian women in the face of extreme adversity.

218. Over 40% of households in Haiti are headed by women, and this percentage has been on the rise since 2007 147, 148. Today, there are more women living under extreme poverty than men, with a large proportion residing in rural areas 149. Most of Haiti’s population is rural, and two-thirds of this population is dependent on agriculture; with women comprising a significant portion of the labour force involved across all aspects of agricultural processes 150.

219. To address gender dimensions within the project design and implementation, the Gender Action Plan will account for:
- specific strategies to include/target female-headed households;
- differing conservation incentives faced by women;
- identification of gaps in gender equality through the use of sex-disaggregated data enabling the gender action plan to close those gaps, devoting resources and expertise for implementing such strategies, monitoring the results of implementation, and holding individuals and institutions accountable for outputs that promote gender equality;
- advocacy and awareness that is adjusted to most effectively reflect gender-specific differences; and
- the inclusion of a Gender Specialist position/provision of advice within the project to implement gender-related activities.

223. The financial management and procurement of this project will be guided by UNDP financial rules and regulations available here¹⁵¹. 226. The national executing entity MoE (also referred to as the national 'Implementing Partner' in UNDP terminology), is required to implement the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations, policies, and procedures (including the NIM guidelines). In legal terms, this is ensured through the national Government’s signature of the UNDP Standard Basic Assistance Agreement (SBAA), together with a UNDP project document which will be signed by the Implementing Partner to govern the use of the funds. Both of these documents require compliance. During implementation, UNDP will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures, and any specific requirements in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and project confirmation to be agreed with the GCF. Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP.

Information provided in confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information.

- ☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

## Secretariat’s assessment of FP208

### Proposal name: Enhanced climate resilience in the Trois-Rivières region of Haiti through Integrated Flood Management

- **Accredited entity:** United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)
- **Country/(ies):** Republic of Haiti
- **Project/programme size:** Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths:
- Direct benefits to 292,600 people (2.7 percent of the total population), targeting the most vulnerable population
- 40 percent reduction in peak run-off with the increase in tree cover (17,740 ha agroforestry and 7,700 ha restoration)
- Reduce floodplain extents by 1 percent AEP

#### Points of caution:
- Ongoing crises including gang-related insecurity could pose a risk that needs to be carefully managed
- Shifting away from unsustainable land-use practices to an integrated and climate-resilient approach to land and water resources management, including community-led governance
- Increasing household incomes, community health outcomes, improved water and soil quality and greater biodiversity, bridging sustainable land use and guaranteeing income
- Economically viable (economic internal rate of return: 22 percent; net present value: USD 38.5 million). Highly efficient with USD 32/beneficiary (direct)

### 2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations", respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
## Page 115  

3. Haiti is a small island developing State (SIDS) and the only least developed country (LDC) in the Caribbean region. The geographical location, heavy dependence on climate-sensitive livelihoods, and high level of longstanding macroeconomic challenges make Haiti highly vulnerable to hydrometeorological disasters. In particular, projected climate change impacts including temperature rise, greater annual rainfall variability, and more frequent and intense hurricanes will lead to increased and more severe flooding events. Between 1975 and 2012, floods, tropical storms and hurricanes resulted in economic damage and losses equivalent to 2 per cent of the country’s gross domestic product on average per year. As such, the Government of Haiti has prioritized watershed management actions in its national adaptation plan in the Northwest and the Aribonite regions where the Trois-Rivières (TR) watershed is located.

4. Although the TR watershed is not the largest watershed in the country, its population is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change-induced flooding, with limited resources to adequately adapt or respond to such events. The rural areas of the TR catchment have an extreme poverty rate of 40 per cent, which is almost twice the national average of 23.9 per cent and much higher than any other area of Haiti. This project will provide a suite of interventions to enhance the resilience of the population and ecosystems of the TR region and promote a transformative approach to the flood management system in the TR watershed.

5. The objective of this project is to enhance the climate resilience of the TR region by initiating a shift away from uncoordinated decision-making and unsustainable land-use practices towards an integrated, climate-resilient approach to land and water resources management. This will be achieved through the following integrated components:  
(a) Ecosystem-based flood management solutions implemented in 25,440 ha of the TR watershed;  
(b) Climate-resilient agricultural practices, optimized value chains and social safety nets established to promote sustainable landscape management and reduce degradation in the TR watershed; and  
(c) Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient integrated water resources management (IWRM).

6. The project will directly benefit at least 292,600 people, of which 52 per cent are women, in the seven target communes across the TR region of Haiti, which accounts for about 3 per cent of the country’s total population. These are the most vulnerable populations of Haiti who have limited adaptive capacity to cope with the increased frequency of flooding and will benefit from the project’s combination of interventions detailed below. The project will also indirectly benefit an additional 441,272 people, of which 52 per cent are women (approximately 4 per cent of the entire population of Haiti).

7. The total project financing is USD 31,290,561, with a request for GCF grants of USD 22,427,206. Heifer International Haiti will provide co-finance of USD 8,303,355 in the form of grants, and United National Development Programme (UNDP) will also provide grant co-financing of USD 560,000. The project is submitted under environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category B.

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis  

### Component 1: Ecosystem-based flood management solutions implemented in 25,440 hectares of the Trois-Rivières watershed (total cost: USD 18.40 million; GCF cost: USD 18.20 million)  

8. Component 1 aims to directly enhance the resilience of the TR catchment by implementing agroforestry systems and restoration of arboreal land cover on degraded mountainous slopes in the TR watershed. An increase in concentrated and heavy rainfall,

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
## Page 116  

combined with the increasing frequency and intensity of hurricanes and tropical storms, has resulted in the rapid erosion of fertile soils throughout the TR watershed. This is exacerbated by the steep topography and highly deforested landscape of the watershed, which intensifies flow rates because of reduced ground cover and further increases erosion and river sedimentation. The combination of these factors often results in the destruction of crops, farmlands and agricultural infrastructure, as well as a loss of livestock and human lives during the flood events.

The implementation of the proposed ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) flood management solutions is expected to address these issues by significantly reducing peak run-off and flooding extent in the area by increasing vegetative cover in the watershed and the infiltration capacity of the soil. More specifically, the agroforestry systems will be implemented in 17,740 ha of degraded land, and reforestation and restoration will be implemented in 7,700 ha of forest areas across the target areas.

### 9. The proposed activities include establishing nurseries, planting seedlings (e.g. for fruit production and reforestation) and providing ongoing technical assistance to end users including on establishing and monitoring of nurseries, scheduling seedling planting, using technical packages for agroforestry and monitoring the agroforestry systems to account for the growing times of many agroforestry species. The spaces between the agroforestry species will be filled with perennial food crops (e.g. plantains, sugar cane, cassava, pigeon peas and pineapple), in combination with annual crops (e.g. sweet potato, yams and other annuals).

### 10. This intervention, which is an example of EbA, will be supplemented by developing the capacity of the target community groups, including farmers’ and women’s associations regarding climate-resilient land-use planning. Thirty-three communities within the seven target communes in the TR watershed will receive this training, which will then result in the development of 33 community land-use plans, through community engagement in a consultative process. These plans will focus on identifying the impacts of climate change, particularly flooding, on the TR watershed and designing appropriate actions for addressing these impacts. Such actions include the implementation of EbA solutions to reduce flood risk in the watershed as well as the promotion of sustainable, climate-resilient land-use practices and techniques to reduce the risk of increased land degradation under future climate change conditions. The farmers in the target areas will receive training on developing sustainable agroforestry business models that will contribute to both improving livelihood security and enhancing the use of sustainable land-use practices. This participatory approach will ensure that the proposed interventions will have buy-in from beneficiary groups.

## Component 2: Climate-resilient agricultural practices, optimized value chains and social safety nets established to promote sustainable landscape management and reduce degradation in the Trois-Rivières watershed (total cost: USD 8.30 million; GCF cost: USD 0)

### 11. Component 2 will facilitate a transformative shift in Haiti’s agricultural sector from unsustainable practices (e.g. the cultivation of annual crop types and clearing of forests for charcoal production) towards sustainable options that are resilient to the impacts of climate change-induced flooding. This shift will result in sustainable livelihoods for the populations of the TR region, while reducing deforestation and land degradation and maintaining the integrity of its land and soil. In turn, this will contribute to decreasing flood damage, and a virtuous cycle will be created in the integrated flood management practices in this region. This component will be entirely funded and implemented by Heifer International for this project with its co-finance funding. A suite of complementary activities will be implemented to achieve this component including: (1) strengthening the institutional capacity of the people who are involved in the technical aspects of agriculture at the target commune, inter-commune and department levels for implementing sustainable and climate-resilient agricultural practices; (2) enhancing access to financial resources for smallholder farmers in order to facilitate the uptake and implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices over the long term; and (3) establishing a social protection system for households that face chronic food insecurity. The co-

# Page 117

## GCF/B.36/02/Add.03

### Component 3: Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient integrated water resources management (total cost: USD 3.27 million; GCF cost: USD 3.27 million)

12. Component 3 will strengthen the capacity of the Government of Haiti to develop and implement the Water Act, which will be an indispensable legal foundation for enabling participatory IWRM in the TR watershed. The current water governance system in Haiti is organized at both the departmental and the communal level, which creates an overlap between different bodies, particularly at the catchment and sub-catchment levels. This poses challenges in coordination and planning among stakeholders at different levels and leads to ineffective and inefficient implementation of an integrated approach to water resources management in the TR watershed.

The activities envisaged under this component include:  
(1) drafting provisions to Haiti’s Water Act for the adoption of an integrated approach to water resources management;  
(2) establishing catchment IWRM committees within 31 catchments in Haiti; and  
(3) establishing sub-catchment water resource users’ associations within the sub-catchments of the 31 catchments.

These committees will work with different stakeholders in the 31 catchments and sub-catchments to manage the efficient use of land and water resources and will be responsible for fostering community empowerment and ownership over these critical resources under future climate change conditions. These activities will ensure that the Water Act is developed and implemented to respond not only to national level priorities, but also to the adaptation needs of vulnerable communities living in the TR watershed.

### Project management (total cost: USD 1.31 million; GCF cost: USD 0.95 million)

13. The GCF portion of this project management cost is less than 5 per cent of the total requested GCF funding and is compliant with the GCF policy on fees.

## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

14. The proposed project will directly benefit 292,600 people (2.7 per cent of the total population), targeting the most vulnerable population.

15. The project net present value (NPV) of interventions is estimated to be USD 207 million with an internal rate of return (IRR) of 37.9 per cent, and therefore represents a highly cost-effective spend for the Government of Haiti. The estimated health benefits under the economic and financial analysis list water-borne diseases (such as diarrhoea and cholera) that would, without the project interventions, likely increase in prevalence because of increased flooding and related impacts.

16. The increase in tree cover (17,740 ha agroforestry and 7,700 ha restoration ) is expected to result in a 40 per cent reduction in peak run-off and reduce floodplain extents by 26 per cent in the areas with a 1 per cent annual exceedance probability (AEP) of floods.

## Page 118  

Making and unsustainable land-use practices towards an integrated, climate-resilient approach to land and water resources management.  

### 18.  
Firstly, agroforestry systems will be established and degraded forests restored at priority intervention sites across the watershed to: (1) reduce peak surface run-off and flood extent; and (2) provide alternative livelihood options to the unsustainable land-use practices on which most vulnerable communities in the TR watershed depend.  

Secondly, complementary interventions focused on enhancing technical and institutional capacity at the commune, inter-commune and department levels will be implemented to promote productive climate-resilient agricultural practices and sustainable land management.  

Finally, the necessary governance framework for IWRM will be established in Haiti to support the uptake of climate-resilient land management systems and to facilitate sustainable use and management of water resources over the long term.  

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential  
#### Scale: N/A  

19. Under the project, the technical and institutional capacity of relevant stakeholders at the national, department and commune levels will be strengthened regarding implementation of an integrated approach to land and water resources management that is centred around sustainability and climate resilience. By building the capacity of the targeted institutions, the transfer of knowledge and skills related to sustainable watershed and flood management will extend beyond the project.  

20. Strengthening the Water Act will establish a legal foundation for optimizing the use of water resources without compromising the sustainability of vital ecosystems under future climate change conditions, consequently securing equitable economic and social welfare. The Water Act will also provide a framework for the institutionalization of integrated watershed management to empower regional administrations across Haiti – through establishing catchment water resources management committees and sub-catchment water resource users associations.  

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient  
#### Scale: N/A  

22. Haiti is a SIDS and an LDC hit by cascading and compounding climate hazards (floods, hurricanes, tropical storms, erosions, etc). The target beneficiaries endure double the national average poverty rate. The watershed is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change-induced flooding. Given the near-complete absence of embankments and levees in the watershed, such flooding causes severe damage to crops and farmland, as well as agricultural infrastructure and equipment. Flooding in the region also has severe consequences for both human and livestock health, resulting in an increased risk of loss of human life and livestock.  

Addressing the impact of climate change-induced flooding on vulnerable communities in the region requires the adoption of an integrated approach to flood management that responds to the impacts of climate change.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
Page 119  

The project is a direct contribution to Haiti’s adaptation targets in relation to watershed management, coastal and marine protection, and reforestation, as reflected in the updated nationally determined contribution submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change in 2022 and responds to the institutional capacity needs identified in Haiti’s national adaptation plan submitted in early 2023. The project is also a prioritized intervention with GCF and is included in the advanced draft country programme which, at the time of writing, was in the last stages of preparation. In this regard the no-objection letter was issued and cleared in April 2022.

Extensive stakeholder consultations were held during the project elaboration and feedback was incorporated into the development of the proposal. Active stakeholder engagement, notably in the intervention areas, is planned to ensure optimal execution of the project and as part of the adaptive management strategy of the project.

The funding proposal package presents economic and financial analyses with reasonable assumptions as well as a clear definition of scope and the counterfactual scenarios. The economic analysis contains estimations of all the relevant economic values from the project activities that are consistent with the information provided in the funding proposal. The model uses a social discount rate of 10 per cent in line with all the other UNDP projects. In summary, the economic model results show the NPV ranging between USD 375 million and USD 412 million with an IRR of 41 per cent and 43 per cent depending on the different flood reduction scenarios. The model shows that the proposed project is economically viable.

The financial analysis has been conducted at the individual farmer’s perspective for the agroforestry activity, assessing whether the farmers would be able to invest in the agroforestry activities described in the funding proposal through their own resources or other existing sources of finance (microfinance, bank loans, etc.) The weighted average cost of capital (WACC) used is 3.7 per cent as the mean of the lowest and highest nominal monthly rates charged by microfinance institutions in peer group and village banks. The financial IRR is above the WACC, although grants are still requested, on the basis of the sensitivity analysis conducted with loan scenarios.

Financial adequacy and appropriate concessionality is assessed to be high. The accredited entity (AE) has conducted a sensitivity analysis for the agroforestry model and calculated financial profitability indicators for the agroforestry model (NPV, financial IRR and payback period) in four cases:  
1. When activities are fully financed by farmers on their own (“without project”);  
2. When activities are financed with GCF grants (“with project”);  
3. When activities are financed with bank loans at the lowest interest rates (2.75 per cent); and  
4. When activities are financed with bank loans at the highest interest rates (4.8 per cent).  

The results show that the agroforestry activities cannot be financed with full bank loans (negative cash flows), and the payback period would be too long for low interest rate loans. An analysis of the mix of loans and grants scenario has not been conducted.

The need for grants in this project is also justified by the public nature of the project activities. The funding proposal describes the absence of financing for the proposed activities for farmers as one of the major barriers. Alternative sources of funding and credit are not available or are difficult to access, leading farmers to go for the option of short-term relief.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  

31. Project background. The project aims to take a sustainable land and water resource management (IWRM) approach as adaptive measures to build resilience in the Trois-Rivières (TR) watershed in Haiti. The main interventions proposed by the project include: i) implementing green infrastructure (agroforestry and restoration activities) to strengthen flood resilience, supported by capacity-building programmes for land-use management; ii) establishing and promoting climate-resilient agricultural and land-use practices to enhance food security in the watershed; and iii) improving national and local governance frameworks for the implementation of integrated water resources management at national, catchment and sub-catchment levels to contribute to creating an enabling environment for climate-resilient flood management in Haiti.  

The project will be implemented through community-based governance and planning mechanisms to be developed in seven communes targeted by the project in the TR watershed, namely Port-de-Paix, Chansolme, Bassin Bleu, Plaisance, Pilate, Marmelade, and Gros Morne. The project will improve climate resilience and food security, decrease the impact of floods, and provide significant environmental and social co-benefits, through ecosystem-based flood management and improved flood management governance.  

32. Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instruments. The AE has classified the project substantial environmental and social (E&S) risk against AE’s own screening criteria. The AE considers the project as equivalent to Category B per GCF’s E&S risk categorization. The project is expected to have potential limited adverse environmental and social risks and/or impacts which are site-specific, largely reversible, and can be mitigated with appropriate environmental and social management measures. Any high-risk activities will be excluded by the project, favouring a climate resilient and green infrastructure approach to flood management, and eschewing high-risk grey infrastructure for flood control.  

The Secretariat confirms that the risk category is aligned with GCF’s E&S risk categorization and is within the E&S risk accreditation level of the AE. The AE has prepared a screening report against its own social and environment screening procedure (SESP) and an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) to guide the E&S screening, assessment and management to be conducted during the project implementation phase, where the preparation of a strategic environmental and social assessment (SESA), site-specific environmental and social impacts assessments (ESIAs) and environmental and social management plans (ESMPs) will be required.

### 39. ESS 4: Community health, safety and security  

The AE has broadly identified that the project activities may generate risks to the health and safety of communities and considered the health impacts related to the flood risk and associated threat to the communities as well as the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. As the small infrastructure and/or equipment to be used at each project site is yet to be identified, the ESMF requires further assessment to be conducted as part of the site-specific ESIA.  

The project will also incorporate community health monitoring as part of the project’s wider efforts of implementing regular monitoring and evaluation of water resources at the catchment and sub-catchment levels (under Activity 3.3 to support the implementation of IWRM plans).  

As it is likely that some of the small infrastructure projects may create noise, dust, or traffic risks for communities, and that if water supply systems are not designed or maintained properly, they may create risk of water- or vector-borne diseases.  

The AE is suggested to provide basic guidance on and include a list of standard mitigation measures for the main types of small-scale infrastructures and equipment likely to be deployed during project implementation.  

### 40. ESS 5: Land acquisition and resettlement  

The AE has triggered its own standard on displacement and resettlement, noting that the project’s agroforestry or reforestation activities (under Activity 1.2, establishing agroforestry systems areas over 17,740 hectares and restoring forestation to 7,700 hectares) can potentially lead to the spatial or temporal restrictions on lands previously used by local communities and on their livelihoods. This could then create economic displacement and pose hardship on local communities.  

The project will exclude activities that will require any physical displacement of people or changes of land tenure.  

The ESMF describes the possibility that land acquisition for small infrastructure investments may emerge during the project implementation, such as those activities that will involve deploying agricultural tracks, developing water infrastructure, and providing upgrades to water infrastructure to address decreasing water security caused by the impacts of climate change-induced flooding.  

The ESMF also notes that in such case the project will only work with those who are willing participants and seeking infrastructural upgrades, and in areas that are dedicated as public lands. The project will ensure that all legislation is followed.  

The ESMF requires that a livelihood action plan (LAP) to be prepared if project activities will cause economic displacement, namely where the livelihoods of individuals or communities are restricted, partially or fully, and either directly or indirectly, in their access to land or resources to support their economic well-being.  

To be developed as part of the ESMP, the LAP will address beneficiary participation in economic displacement decision-making, adequate (full and fair) compensation and assistance, and risk management to ensure that livelihoods are at least as good as prior to the program implementation and that the livelihoods of poor and marginalized are improved.  

The LAP will include independent monitoring procedures, clarification of land rights consistent with applicable law, and outline capacity, training, and development actions targeting beneficiaries’ livelihoods.

### Linkage between resettlement implementation and initiation of civil works for each of the project components

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources  
As listed in the project’s exclusionary criteria, the project will exclude project activities that will be implemented in areas of critical habitats, unless all of the following are demonstrated. That is, (i) there are no measurable adverse impacts on the criteria or biodiversity values for which the critical habitat was designated, and on the ecological processes supporting those biodiversity values (determined on an ecologically-relevant scale); (ii) there is no reduction of any recognized Endangered, Vulnerable or Critically Endangered species; (iii) any lesser impacts are mitigated; and (iv) a robust, appropriately designed, and long-term Biodiversity Action Plan is in place to achieve net gains of those biodiversity values for which the critical habitat was designated.  

The project will exclude activities that involve the application of chemical fertilizers or pesticides as a result of the agroforestry and reforestation interventions of the project, as well as activities that risk the introduction of alien and potentially invasive alien species. Despite such exclusion, the ESMF identifies that the project could expose substantial risks on biodiversity due to that Port-de-Paix, one of the targeted project sites, is a KBA and an Alliance for Zero Extinction (AZE) site. This KBA falls within the project’s AREA 2 encompassing the semiarid areas across Gros-morne, Chansolme, Port-de-Paix, and Bassin Bleu.  

The AE expects that the community land use plan for this area (under Sub activity 1.1.1) may be developed potentially within or adjacent to this KBA. The ESMF requires that the decision as to whether project activities will take place within or adjacent to this KBA and other critical habitats must follow the mitigation hierarchy as described in the ESMF. If project activities take place within or adjacent to a KBA, those activities must be guided by an appropriately scoped and detailed Biodiversity Action Plan (BAP) to create positive impacts on biodiversity conservation and in line with the requirements of AE’s relevant and guidance.  

The project will assess its potential impacts on biodiversity and natural resources more clearly at the start of the project implementation, through conducting a SESA and site-specific ESIAs. Depending on the scope of the project activities to be designed at the site-level, mitigation measures will be formulated as part of the site-specific ESMP or as a standalone Biodiversity Management Plan (BMP). The ESMF provides detailed guidance for the development of context specific BAP or BMP to ensure that the project will achieve net gains to the biodiversity values and underpinning ecosystem services for which the critical habitat was designated.  

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples  
Based on a review of UNDPs’ and other agencies’ programmes in Haiti and field consultations carried out by the AE, the AE concluded that the are no sub-group in the project area and area of influence that meet the criteria for indigenous peoples (e.g. has pursued its own way or developed distinct group identity, languages or no distinct ethnicity nor group self-identifying as indigenous peoples in Haiti or no special connection between Haitian people and their territory other than the national one). Accordingly, the AE concluded that the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and its own Social and Environmental Standard 6 were not triggered.  

### ESS 8: Cultural heritage  
The ESMF identifies moderate risk to cultural heritage that may arise from undertaking project activities that will involve the movement of earth over large areas. This may lead to the accidental discovery of or damage to cultural heritage. The ESMF provides chance finds procedures to be followed at site- and activity-level in all plans and contracts. The procedures will be applied to project-related construction works, including excavations, demolitions, movement of earth, flooding, or other changes in the physical environment.  

The procedures establish how chance finds of tangible cultural heritage shall be managed, including notification of relevant authorities and stakeholders, avoidance of further disturbance or damage, protection, documentation, and assessment of found objects by relevant experts. Per AE’s own standard, the procedures will be applied in the case of archaeological sites.

### 43. Cultural heritage  

The ESMF has identified the project’s moderate risks on cultural heritage. The project will ensure that through the site-specific ESMPs a chance finds procedure will be implemented during all works of the project. The ESMF requires that the community participation, stakeholder consultations and use of experts will be exercised fully and effectively to deal with cultural heritage properties, and that all discoveries and any archaeological sites discovered by the project will be immediately reported to local authorities.  

### 44. Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harassment (SEAH) safeguarding  

The ESMF has identified the project’s moderate risks on gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH. The project plans to provide significant support to women through enhancing their access to natural resources and increasing their ability to participate and derive equal benefits from project-related outcomes, including the outcomes targeting livelihoods. While the project expects to increase women’s income through project-sponsored activities, the traditional power dynamics in the household and in a broader societal context could be changed and that the gender norms and an existing culture of discrimination can be challenged. Such shifts can lead to and exacerbate the existing GBV and SEAH.  

The AE has conducted an initial SEAH risk assessment and identified the risk factors, indicators, and mitigation measures related to preventing SEAH (PSEAH). Based on the PSEAH safeguarding approach taken at the project-level, the ESMF requires that in-depth SEAH risk assessment to be undertaken as part of the site-specific ESIAs. Subsequently, the corresponding PSEAH measures to prevent, mitigate and respond to the SEAH risks for each site will be specified in the ESMPs.  

The project will establish a survivor-centred grievance redress mechanism (GRM) for SEAH-related grievances and complaints in line with good practices identified in the ESMF. The project will hire a gender and safeguards specialist to guide project safeguards compliance on SEAH.  

### 45. Implementation arrangements  

The Ministry of Environment of Haiti will act as the executing entity (EE) for this project and will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU). The PMU will comprise of a national project manager, who will be supported by specialists in environment, social development, and gender and social inclusion, as well as technical specialists.  

The PMU will be responsible for supervising and managing the implementation of measures defined in the ESMF, assign specific responsibilities within the implementing partner for the ESMF implementation, report to the AE and the Project Steering Committee on the implementation of the ESMF, and ensure that all service providers are informed of their responsibilities for the day-to-day compliance with the ESMF.  

The AE will provide quality assurance for the project, ensure the project’s compliance with the AE’s policies and procedures, including its own E&S standards, and ensure the implementation of the requirements set forth in the ESMF.  

### 46. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure  

The ESMF outlines the overall requirements on stakeholder engagement to be followed during project implementation. The details of the existing stakeholder consultations are summarized in a stakeholder consultation and engagement plan provided separately by the AE, that includes a stakeholder analysis and provides an SEP to be followed at the site-level during project implementation.  

The SEP outlines processes and mechanisms for continued consultations with all the above stakeholders during project implementation, with targeted communication methods for different stakeholders. The SEP also includes specific outreach mechanisms for women and disadvantaged/ vulnerable groups in the engagement plan.  

The stakeholders will be assured access to relevant project information to understand the potential project-related opportunities and risks and to engage in the detailed project design and implementation.  

The project will disclose information on the potential project-related E&S impacts and mitigation/management measures in advance of decision-making whenever possible. As the project risk has been assessed as substantial, the project will follow the disclosure framework specified in the ESMF to ensure that all the E&S safeguards documentation will be disclosed and consulted on prior to implementation of activities that may give rise to potential adverse E&S impacts.

## 47. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM)  
The ESMF provides the principles for the development of a project-level GRM and a compliant register, which will handle community-related issues during construction works. Any complaint and/or grievance will be advised to the AE and EE within 24 hours of receipt. The ESMF requires a summary list of complaints and/or grievances received, and their disposition to be published in a report produced every six months.  

The project-level GRM will be operationalized at the start of the project implementation to address all complaints and/or grievances in the project. The stakeholders will be informed of the various means for making complaints and/or grievances, including in addition to the project-level and national grievance redress mechanisms, the option to access AE’s own accountability mechanism that has both the compliance and grievance functions.  

A social and environmental compliance unit hosted by the AE’s office of audit and investigations will investigate allegations regarding the AE's standards, screening procedure, or other E&S commitments that are not being implemented adequately, and that harm may result to people or the environment. A compliance review is available to any community or individual with concerns about the impacts of the AE’s programmes or projects. The unit is mandated by the AE to independently and impartially investigate valid requests from locally impacted people, and to report its findings and recommendations publicly.  

The ESMF also includes the GCF’s Independent Redress Mechanism as an integral of the project’s GRM.  

## 4.2 Gender policy  
### 48.  
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The assessment was informed by secondary data and outcomes from the consultations during mission visits by the project development team.  

### 49.  
The climate change and disaster risk management legislative frameworks in Haiti do not adequately address the issues of gender equality and the empowerment of women. However, flooding and other natural disasters have cumulative livelihood effects that unfairly impact women and exacerbate gender inequality. Evidence and impacts were drawn from the 2010 earthquake and 2015 Hurricane Matthew.  

In Haiti, women are disproportionately represented among the poor and there is a significant proportion of women-headed households, so impacts such as crop loss, livestock drowning, destroyed homes or displacement, and health risks and loss of life due to floods ultimately affect women first and foremost.  

Gender-based violence is another issue that normally goes unnoticed and unreported, including police violence targeting women and marginalized groups. Of the cases of violence reported between 2009 and 2011, the majority were physical in nature (59.74 per cent), followed by sexual violence (29.80 per cent), while cases of psychological and economic violence constituted a minority (5.88 per cent and 4.58 per cent, respectively).  

### 50.  
From this gender assessment, various gender-responsive approaches have been proposed, including building a strong collaboration with public water resources departments in ensuring that the design and installation of water infrastructure facilities are based on gender assessments; increasing the capacity of local government on women’s leadership and participation in water management; supporting local municipal authorities and communities to develop and maintain gender-equitable distribution of water resources; and ensuring that partnerships with the private sector include a commitment to gender equality and that women receive equal access to technical or management training, peer-to-peer learning and other resources.  

Other gender recommendations have been made in the areas of agricultural and climate change resilience, reforestation and agroforestry systems, including promoting land ownership and other productive resources in forest management and climate mitigation strategies.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
Page 126  

## 51. A gender and safeguards expert (at programme management unit level) will be responsible for the implementation of the gender action plan (GAP) in collaboration with Heifer International (the EE for outcome 2). Gender technical expertise and assistance will also be sought on an ad hoc basis to supplement the available skill set and experience within the programme management unit. The expected outcome of the GAP is improved capacities for an estimated 87,780–146,300 women (30–50 per cent of project direct beneficiaries) on sustainable water and land management as a means to enhance their resilience to climate change. Some of the activities outlined in the GAP include (1) strengthening the capacity of community groups, farmer and women’s associations in climate-resilient and land-use management; (2) including gender modules during training sessions for communities and farmer associations in the seven targeted communes on the application of community land-use plans and implementation of climate resilient land-use practices (50 per cent of trainers will be women); (3) establishing commune-level development councils and multi-sectoral management structures to oversee and manage the implementation of project interventions, including strongly encouraging women’s leadership in these institutions (with a target of 50 per cent leadership and participation by women); and (4) drafting the provisions of Haiti’s Water Act for the adoption and implementation of an integrated approach to water resources management in the TR watershed, including provisions on gender responsiveness to ensure inclusive water governance and other inclusive clauses at the policy level.  

## 4.3 Risks  
### 4.3.1. Overall programme assessment (medium risk)  
52. The GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 22.9 million, which will be invested to address climate change-induced flood impacts in the TR watershed and the impacts on population residing nearby. Heifer International Haiti and the AE will provide co-financing of USD 8.3 million and USD 0.5 million, respectively. The AE is requesting full grant support for this project. As an LDC and SIDS, Haiti has been experiencing increases in public debt which is placing the country at a high risk of debt distress.  

### 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)  
53. UNDP is the AE for the project. UNDP Haiti country office has been working in Haiti since 1973 and has experiences implementing water and land use-related climate change projects through policy formulation and strategic planning. The country office has a track record of managing projects with an EbA approach, and these projects have had a budget range of between USD 2 million and USD 15 million in recent years.  

54. The Ministry of Environment (MoE) is the EE of this project. UNDP has carried out a capacity assessment of the EE, which concluded an overall moderate risk with a significant risk regarding monitoring and evaluation (M&E), and procurement and financial management. Requested by the Government of Haiti, UNDP will provide direct project services by assisting procurement processes and making payments to the suppliers and responsible parties at the instruction of MoE.  

### 4.3.3. Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)  
55. The AE has identified the weak land tenure and management system as one of the risks that could delay or prevent the implementation of the project. The feasibility study stated that there are many constraints in terms of obtaining land titles, fragmented land holdings and legal provisions limiting the use of land as collateral, and so on. The current mitigation measure

(a) Financial sustainability beyond the project implementation period may be achieved through the Village Saving and Credit Association (VSCA) and the budgetary contribution from the national government. Given the budgetary constraints of the Haitian Government, its financial commitment is uncertain and there is no co-financing provided by the Government. (b) The AE carried out an economic analysis that identified an economic IRR of 41 per cent and 43 per cent, depending on the choice of flood reduction scenarios, over 25 years. The analysis considered the benefits from the reduction of water-borne diseases (health benefits), reduction of infrastructure damage, and increase in the livelihood income. With regards to the livelihood benefits, a financial analysis has been conducted at an individual farmer’s perspective for the agroforestry activity (see paragraph 28 above). The analysis shows that the agroforestry activity cannot be financed with loans and farmers will have difficult in accessing additional financing such as credit. Therefore, the full grant support is justified. As noted above, the project viability will also depend on the capacity of the national government to take over the operation and management of the project, and the analysis assumed that there would be no additional operation and management costs beyond project implementation.

82. The UNDP will be the AE of the project. It will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures, and any specific requirements in the accreditation master agreement (AMA) and project confirmation to be agreed with the GCF.

These include relevant requirements on fiduciary, procurement, ESS and other performance standards. 85. UNDP will also provide implementation support in the delivery of activities included within the project’s output 3. 86. Heifer International Haiti will act as a responsible party for output 1. It will execute all activities under output 2 through its own co-finance. # GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
## Page 135  

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of  
FP208  

### Proposal name: Enhanced climate resilience in the Trois-Rivières region of Haiti through Integrated Flood Management  

#### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)  
#### Country/(ies): Haiti  
#### Project/programme size: Small  

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

1. This funding proposal for a small-sized public sector adaptation project in environmental and social safeguards category B, to be implemented over an 8-year period and with benefits accruing over a 25-year project lifespan, is submitted by the Government of Haiti (GoH) through the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) as accredited entity (AE), with the Ministry of Environment of Haiti (MoE) as the executing entity (EE). The total cost of the funding proposal (FP) is USD 31.29 million, of which USD 22.43 million is requested from GCF, with USD 8.86 million in co-finance from Heifer International Haiti (Heifer) and UNDP.  

2. The proposed project will contribute to GCF’s paradigm shift objective of increased climate-resilient sustainable development by enhancing the resilience of Haiti’s Trois-Rivières (TR) watershed and its communities to the impacts of climate change intensified flooding. This shift will be achieved by implementing a transformative approach to the way in which the GoH and farming communities address flood impacts, incorporating ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA), and integrated land and water resource planning and management that is informed by climate risk. Specifically, direct investments from GCF combined with co-financing from Heifer will be used for:  

- **output 1**: Ecosystem-based flood management solutions implemented in 25,440 hectares of the Trois-Rivières watershed: activities to implement agroforestry systems and rehabilitate ‘water towers’ through reforestation of degraded landscapes at priority intervention sites; also including climate change awareness with local government and communities, and participatory land-use planning;  
- **output 2**: Climate-resilient agricultural practices, optimized value chains and social safety nets established to promote sustainable land management and reduce degradation in the TR watershed – activities to enhance technical and institutional capacity for productive climate-resilient land management at the national and local levels, including support to tree nurseries, agri-processing cooperatives and village savings and credit associations (VSCAs);  
- **output 3**: Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient integrated water resources management (IWRM) – activities to establish the required governance framework, to support the climate-resilient land and water management, including amendments to the Water Act, and establishment of catchment committees and sub-catchment water user associations across the country.  

3. The third component involves capacity-building at the national level and at catchment level throughout the country, and the other two components will focus only on the TR watershed.

watershed in the country’s north-west, which is highly vulnerable to floods, that are expected to become more frequent and severe with the changing climate. This work will be carried out in 33 communal sections with 118 communities. These communities are based in seven target communes, falling into three departments – Port-de-Paix, Chansolme and Bassin-Bleu (Northwest); Plaisance and Pilate (North); and Marmelade and Gros-Morne (Artibonite).  

### 1.1 Impact potential                                      Scale: N/A  

#### 4. Climate change basis: The FP highlights Haiti’s extreme vulnerability to climate change impacts, as well as earthquakes (which are generally considered natural disasters). In the past two decades, Haiti has experienced 26 floods, 12 of which were catastrophic, 8 hurricanes, and numerous other tropical storms and earthquakes, with severe damage and loss caused by climate change intensified flooding in particular. For example, in 2004, Hurricane Jeanne caused severe flooding in the northern part of Haiti, resulting in more than 2,800 deaths and approximately USD 41 million in damage. More recently, Hurricane Matthew struck Haiti in 2016, causing 546 deaths and the loss of up to 90 per cent of crops and livestock in some areas.  

#### 5. The FP and feasibility study (FS) use historical data and future projections to demonstrate convincingly that climate change is causing an increase in annual average temperatures and creating the conditions for increased frequency and intensity of hurricanes in the Caribbean region. Climate change is causing a decrease in total annual rainfall in Haiti, but also a shortening of the wet season, with a resulting increase in concentration and intensity of rainfall events. These changing climatic conditions are contributing to an increase in the frequency and intensity of flooding in the country, particularly in the north-western TR watershed of the Massif du Nord, with its steep topography.  

#### 6. The FP also shows how climate change impacts are compounded by direct human pressures on the environment, with degraded landscapes caused by unsustainable agricultural practices, and historical deforestation, that involved felling of 99 per cent of the primary forest cover of the country, much of this for charcoal production. During heavy rainfall events, degraded areas experience increased surface run-off, worsened by decreased infiltration of water into soil because of reduced vegetation cover. These factors, combined with steep slopes and the lack of embankments along the river, increase the flow of water into the low-lying plains, causing flooding of downstream villages. Increased water flow intensifies erosion and causes sediment deposits in the riverbed and drainage infrastructure, further exacerbating flooding, including in the downstream city of Port de Paix. Drinking water contaminated with sewage and pollutants, and pools of standing floodwater increase the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases.  

#### 7. Adaptation impact: The project aims to deliver impacts against three GCF adaptation results areas in the target communes of the TR watershed:  
(a) Increased resilience of most vulnerable people and communities;  
(b) Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security; and  
(c) Increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services.  

##### 1 Although 99 per cent of Haiti’s original forest cover and its high biodiversity was lost, significant regreening has occurred in recent years through smallholder production of fast-growing tree species for use as fuelwood and sale into charcoal value chains – with up to 36 per cent of the country now ‘reforested’ in this manner, and harvested on a sustainable basis using coppicing techniques. See https://www.climatelinks.org/sites/default/files/asset/document/2021-06/2021_USAID-ProLand-Haiti-Woodfuel-Case-Study-Final.pdf

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
## Page 137  

8. Overall, the project will confer direct and indirect adaptation benefits to approximately 292,600 and 441,272 vulnerable people in Haiti, respectively. By implementing 17,740 ha of agroforestry systems and rehabilitating 7,700 ha of forests in priority areas in the TR watershed to address the impacts of climate change-induced flooding, it is anticipated that the interventions will reduce peak surface run-off and floodplain extents, resulting in a 35 per cent reduction in the number of households at risk from 20-year flood events, and a more than 50 per cent reduction in the number of households affected by 100-year flood events. This will be achieved through revegetation which i) restores the ecosystems’ capacity for water infiltration and reduces flash floods; ii) reduces the soil erosion that worsens downstream flooding through siltation of drainage structures; and, although this is less emphasized in the FP, iii) improves groundwater availability and baseflow2 in the dry season. Agroforestry interventions will enhance 15,000 vulnerable households’ resilience and improve food security, by diversifying and increasing agricultural production and adding value through agri-processing support.  

9. Interventions to reduce the extent of flooding are expected to reduce the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases, including, inter alia, cholera, diarrhoea and typhoid, with an expert hired to monitor disease incidence across the target communes, assisted by water user associations. The agroforestry and reforestation interventions under this project are also expected to result in an important co-benefit of reducing greenhouse gas emissions. The combined project investments will transform Haiti’s approach to land and water resources management and create an enabling environment for the uptake of climate-resilient, sustainable flood management practices in the public sector and in farming communities. The project aims to create pathways to scale up and replicate these practices in catchments across Haiti through activating catchment management structures.  

10. Adaptation options and impact: The project proponents conducted an options analysis to choose between ‘grey’ engineered solutions to flooding, such as embankments, check dams, canals and discharge channels on the one hand, and ‘green’ ecosystems-based solutions, such as tree planting, natural regeneration, slope stabilization, riverbank vegetation, and contour line farming, on the other. Green solutions were selected because they are less costly and require less complex and expensive maintenance, and also because of the wide range of co-benefits they provide – for food security and human health, ecosystem health and biodiversity.  

11. The justification for the selection of an EBA approach is set out in the FS, though it is noted that less technical detail is provided on the green solutions in section 4 in the options analysis. This is true also of section 7, which provides much less detail on the means of catchment reforestation, in comparison with the technical guidance on agroforestry. In answer to a question from the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) about how detailed protocols would be developed for reforestation, as well as monitoring and community engagement, the AE responded that an operations manual would be developed in year 2 of the project, in collaboration with participating communities. The iTAP welcomes the planned annual monitoring of dry season baseflow at four locations along the main stem of the TR river – to provide an indicator of improvements in soil infiltration capacity as a result of upstream reforestation. Technical monitoring protocols will be developed as part of the manual, using a low-tech "infiltration rate tool" and “water flow metering” techniques to “increase community engagement and limit technical dependence/maintenance without compromising accuracy of data”.  

12. In response to a question about how the project’s actual results in mitigating flood risk will be tracked, the AE highlighted the significant budget allocation (through Heifer and UNDP co-finance) for impact analysis, evaluation and studies to monitor results – including, if/when a flood does occur, conducting post-flood community surveys in the project intervention sites, and integrating this with official data gathered by government and United Nations disaster risk

### Teams  
The hydrographic modelling outlined in the FS will be updated, both with geographical information system information to be collected in year 1 as a baseline, and with a full hydrographic analysis in year 8, to be presented to government for future planning and replication in other areas of the country.

### Conflict sensitivity analysis  
In response to a query about the potential for project impacts to be limited by the country’s fragile security situation, with lawlessness in and around Port-au-Prince, and gangs now controlling much of Haiti’s territory, UNDP indicated that the activities are located in relatively secure parts of Haiti, with the partial exception of Gros-Morne and Bassin-Bleu. Nonetheless, it is acknowledged that “the security issue should be considered a destabilizing factor that makes the operating context more prone to conflict and increases risks.” To mitigate the impact of the security situation and maximise the peacebuilding potential of the project, a proactive approach to conflict sensitivity will be adopted and will be promoted by UNDP. This will begin with a detailed conflict analysis of project sites as outlined in the environmental and social management framework (section 6.1.6). A security plan and a conflict assessment will be developed for each commune, as part of the community land-use planning process, that “will map issues and dynamics that bring groups together and divide them, particularly with regard to natural resources, and assess more deeply how the project may interact with local conflict dynamics.” Procurement logistics are planned to circumvent the capital city, and project benefits are not provided in cash, in order to reduce the risk that beneficiaries and staff are targeted by criminal gangs in the delivery of project activities.

### The iTAP appreciates the conflict sensitivity analysis conducted  
and the process to continue conflict assessment and mitigation during project implementation. We note that work on the ground in the north-west (i.e. components 1 and 2, and outputs 3.2 and 3.3), will be supported with supplies from the region or airlifted in by the United Nations Humanitarian Air Service, with no supplies coming by road from Port-au-Prince, which is worst affected by gang violence and roadblocks. Having a project management office in the local directorate of the MoE in Port-de-Paix will also mean that staff do not need to travel from the capital in the south, and that they will help to enable effective delivery. It is noted that similar arrangements are in place for a number of other rural projects in the MoE–UNDP environment portfolio, which currently has a 90 per cent delivery rate.

### We remain concerned  
however, about the ability of the project to carry out the national level work of output 3.1 effectively, strengthening the national governance framework for IWRM and scaling up the approach by establishing catchment and sub-catchment management structures in 31 catchments across the country. This aspect, important for maximizing adaptation impact, would appear very difficult to achieve under the current conditions, with travel by MoE officials from the capital restricted. Even without such restrictions, it would be challenging to establish new structures in other catchments, without the kind of in-depth capacity development happening in the TR catchment. Under the current security situation, it would seem near impossible.

### Overall  
the project’s adaptation impact in reducing flood risk is dependent on the success of the reforestation and agroforestry efforts, as well as the development of the governance framework for climate-risk informed land and water management, all of which are taking place in a challenging socio-economic and security context. The work on the ground in the TR watershed may have a medium to high impact potential, while the impact potential of output 3.1 may be low, given the anticipated challenges in implementation. The iTAP assesses the impact potential of the project as medium overall.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
## Page 139  

### 17. Overall paradigm shift:  
The proposed project intends to catalyse a paradigm shift in Haiti’s approach to managing climate change intensified flooding in the TR watershed and beyond – by initiating a shift away from the present fragmented, uncoordinated decision-making and unsustainable land-use practices towards an integrated approach to land and water resource management that takes into account the current and projected impacts of climate change. Without GCF support, the GoH will continue to operate under the existing paradigm of reactive recovery from the impacts of climate change intensified disasters, including severe flooding, and failure to prepare for slow-onset events like drought. The project aims to incentivize this shift and make it sustainable through an integrated approach – restoring strategic areas of the TR catchment (C1), while promoting sustainable incomes based on regenerative agroforestry (C2) and building a governance framework that supports rural committees to plan land use and manage catchments effectively for climate risk (C3).  

### 18. The lead role of MoE in executing the project, and the strong emphasis on governance and capacity development for local government, national government and community structures, help to embed the integrated land and water management approach to climate change adaptation for the long term. A pathway to scaling up is built into the project through output 3.1, although The iTAP notes the likely challenges that will be faced in carrying out national government level work, and work across all catchments nationwide, given the security context. The intended scale-up involves amendments to Haiti’s draft Water Act to include, inter alia, provisions for the adoption of IWRM structures across the country, and the subsequent establishment of 31 new catchment IWRM committees, each with sub-catchment water resource users associations. In terms of sustainability, the iTAP notes the commitment of MoE to develop and sustain IWRM structures, and its letter to GCF, committing USD 7.5 million for post-project operations and maintenance (O&M), supporting the “maintenance of the cost of the green infrastructure set up by the project” (annex 22).  

### 19. Challenges of reforestation:  
The iTAP posed a question to the AE about whether the intended paradigm shift might be overambitious, particularly the attempts to reforest the TR catchment on a sustainable basis. In the context of Haiti’s extreme development challenges, many past reforestation efforts have failed to sustain tree growth, and stakeholder structures set up to support these efforts have often collapsed once donor funds dry up. The AE acknowledged the depth of the challenges, but pointed to the systematic analysis conducted by UNDP and MoE of 22 past and current related initiatives (as shown in the FS) to understand the elements of success and failure, and to design the proposed project accordingly. Based on the written responses and verbal interactions with the AE, the iTAP has identified three key elements built into the project design that improve the chances of achieving the desired shift, despite the difficult circumstances:  

#### (a) Agroforestry business as an incentive:  
Despite decades of support to agroforestry, many farming communities maintain a focus on annual crops, because of the short-term returns, and a lack of access to finance. In the proposed project’s output 1.2, in addition to GCF investment in reforesting 7,700 water towers (USD 5 million), the largest single portion of the GCF investment (USD 11 million) will go into best-practice agroforestry support on 17,740 ha of farmers’ own land (including women-headed households). Technical assistance will focus on maximizing the use of native and exotic tree species with value for fruit, medicine and fodder, in combination with food crops, for which they provide shade and soil enrichment, as well as support for beekeeping and small livestock. The Heifer co-finance in outputs 2.1 and 2.2 will sustain this investment by enabling commercial agroforestry as a long-term incentive for keeping trees standing – through technical support to farmer field schools, promotion of cooperative nurseries and agri-processing facilities, and access to appropriate micro-loans through VSCAs.  

#### (b) A transition model underpinning the paradigm shift for the agroforestry interventions is depicted in the figure below taken from the FP, demonstrating how the proposed eight-

### (c) Social capital approach to community engagement: While many past reforestation efforts have worked hard to engage communities in tree planting, and sometimes also in monitoring tree survival, the approach to community engagement has not necessarily been a bottom-up approach, driven primarily by communities’ own needs and knowledge. As explained by the AE, “[p]ast projects have mainly been guided by top-down directives that, while taking into account socialization with the communities, have not made them co-participants in the design of interventions that will be made within their own lands. This in turn has resulted in lack of ownership and sustainability as well as success of the forestry investment themselves”

### 4.  
### (d) By contrast, the proposed project has been designed around a social capital approach,5 in which community engagement is focused explicitly on building social cohesion and shared value, as a sound basis for sustaining project interventions. Both reforestation and agroforestry interventions will be designed to take place on and in walking distance of farming households’ own lands, and will be guided by community land-use plans developed through a participatory process, with community-based agreements to be signed governing reforestation plantings and their long-term security. A diverse range of agroforestry species will be used, to be selected by farmers themselves based on traditional usage and preferences. Intensive capacity development will focus on understanding the tangible benefits of the interventions to the communities – in terms

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.03  
## Page 141  

The principles of the farmer field school approach will be built into the reforestation work (as well as the agroforestry), with farmer’s association and women’s groups, guided by locally based agronomists and field technicians, learning surveying and monitoring skills, and taking long-term responsibility for the areas of land designated by themselves through the land-use plans as having value through reforestation.  

### (e) In answer to the iTAP question about how restoration work outside people’s own farms will be incentivized, the AE explained that the project will apply the traditional ‘Kombit’ system to mobilize communal labour for tree planting, irrigation and agri-processing, which, according to the AE “has proven to be effective not only in delivering project impacts but also in enhancing social cohesion and ownership”6. This is reinforced by Heifer’s proven ‘Passing of the Gift (pase kado)’ approach, in which beneficiaries are required to “pass on the gift” to their community through capacity-building and support for replication. “This system has been used by Heifer successfully in various type of projects and is a key part of the project’s sustainability strategy, as it will create a sense of local ownership, community and appropriation and allowing for the implementation of community best practices that are locally appropriate”7.  

Stakeholder consultations demonstrated that this approach would be welcomed and would be seen as less of a top-down approach than that taken in many past projects. Additional incentive measures that have proven to be successful include competitions around environmental protection and monitoring, and special campaigns with students and women’s associations that are involved in the protection of seedlings in public areas.  

### (f) Funding enabling conditions, not running costs: A number of past initiatives in Haiti have set up their own project-specific multi-stakeholder structures and funded their operations, only to find that these structures have not survived long-term (see useful analysis on this in a May 2021 report on the USAID Haiti Reforestation Project). The proposed project utilizes structures that already exist, or for which legal provision is already made by government. This includes Heifer’s support in output 2.1 for existing communal development councils in the seven target communes to produce community land-use plans, and GCF support in outputs 3.1 and 3.2 for a new (but required by law) Trois Rivières Catchment Water Resources Management Committee and sub-catchment water resource user associations. Most importantly, the project will not provide for the running costs of these organizations, which will largely be expected to be supplied in kind by local government and by community organizations (e.g. producer associations, women’s groups). Instead, the project invests in creating the necessary enabling conditions for these structures to operate and bring value to communities, such that their continued existence is enabled.  

### (g) As indicated by the AE to the iTAP: “As evident in the project’s budget, a key part of the project’s sustainability approach is in fact not covering the full cost of the community structures that will be supported by the project, rather creating the enabling conditions (feasibility studies, supporting the creation of common bylaws, networking, expert support, covering costs related to training workshops of their members) to allow these to operate and link their work towards sustainable water and land management as evidence[d] by the various benefits that will be created (improved health, food security, water supply, access to financial capital, diversified livelihoods)... The project further will follow MoE rules on the ground that are applied when there is no project, and avoid

### (h) This approach also applies to the enabling conditions for VSCAs, selected by the project proponents as the best way to support producers (through Heifer co-finance) to invest continuously in agroforestry, rather than outside microcredit provision, which the AE convincingly demonstrates is not affordable in this context. ### 20. Food coupon strategy: An interesting element of the project’s theory of change in bringing about the paradigm shift relates to the provision of food coupons (exchangeable for produce in local markets) to 934 highly food-insecure families in the project sites, using Heifer co-finance in output 2.2 for a temporary (eight-year) intervention. Following a query from the iTAP, the AE indicated that this is planned as a transition measure: “The logic behind the food coupons responds to a key driver of deforestation, which is the need to address short term food insecurity as a result of flood-related losses, through the selling of charcoal as a livelihood option… providing access to a basket of produce to address immediate food insecurity needs, while allowing the space required for the agroforestry interventions to take hold”. In response to concerns about sustainability and potential market distortions, the AE explained that these are addressed by gradually phasing out the coupons from year 6, and by designing the mechanism to work in association with local food producers, helping to create a market for the newly developed products being supported by the project.

Timber (poles, planks, fuelwood) for household use is also envisaged.

This could include awareness of the negative impacts of traditional charcoal production, and the extent to which these can be mitigated through the use of improved low-emission charcoal kilns (for production), as well as fuel-efficient and/or cleaner cookstoves (for consumption).

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential    Scale: N/A  

24. Overall, the project’s paradigm shift potential is significant, and mechanisms have been put in place to improve the chances of sustainable outcomes on the ground in the project sites. Replication and scale-up may be difficult, however, given the challenges noted in section 1.1 above, around work in other catchments.
